[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through the given list of numbers.
2. For each number, iterate through the list again and calculate the absolute difference between the current number and every other number.
3. If the absolute difference is less than the given threshold, return True.
4. If no such pair of numbers is found, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def has_close_elements(numbers: List[float], threshold: float) -> bool:
    for i in range(len(numbers)):
        for j in range(i+1, len(numbers)):
            if abs(numbers[i] - numbers[j]) < threshold:
                return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to separate the groups of balanced parentheses in the input string.
2. We can achieve this by iterating over the characters in the string and keeping track of the opening and closing parentheses.
3. Whenever we encounter an opening parenthesis '(', we start a new group and capture characters until we encounter a closing parenthesis ')'.
4. We can ignore any spaces in the input string while processing.
5. Return the list of separated groups.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def separate_paren_groups(paren_string: str) -> List[str]:
    groups = []
    current_group = ""
    in_group = False

    for char in paren_string:
        if char == '(':
            in_group = True
        elif char == ')':
            in_group = False
            groups.append(current_group)
            current_group = ""
        elif in_group and char != ' ':
            current_group += char

    return groups
```

```
from typing import List

def below_zero(operations: List[int]) -> bool:
    balance = 0
    for operation in operations:
        balance += operation
        if balance < 0:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To calculate the Mean Absolute Deviation (MAD) for a given list of numbers, we first need to calculate the mean of the numbers.
2. Then, for each number in the list, we calculate the absolute difference between the number and the mean.
3. We take the average of all these absolute differences to get the MAD.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def mean_absolute_deviation(numbers: List[float]) -> float:
    # Calculate the mean
    mean = sum(numbers) / len(numbers)
    
    # Calculate the absolute differences and store them in a list
    absolute_diff = [abs(num - mean) for num in numbers]
    
    # Calculate the average of the absolute differences
    mad = sum(absolute_diff) / len(absolute_diff)
    
    return mad
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function named `intersperse` that takes two arguments - a list of integers `numbers` and an integer `delimeter`.
2. Initialize an empty list to store the result.
3. Iterate through the input list `numbers` and for each element:
    - Append the element to the result list.
    - If the current element is not the last element in the list, append the `delimeter` to the result list.
4. Return the result list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def intersperse(numbers: List[int], delimeter: int) -> List[int]:
    result = []
    for i in range(len(numbers)):
        result.append(numbers[i])
        if i != len(numbers) - 1:
            result.append(delimeter)
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to parse the input string which contains multiple groups of nested parentheses separated by spaces.
2. For each group, we have to determine the deepest level of nesting of parentheses.
3. To achieve this, we can iterate over the groups, count the opening and closing parentheses to determine the maximum depth.
4. We will maintain a variable to store the current depth while iterating through the parentheses of a group.
5. Whenever we encounter an opening parentheses '(', we increment the depth, and when we encounter a closing parentheses ')', we decrement the depth.
6. We keep track of the maximum depth encountered within a group and store it in the result list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def parse_nested_parens(paren_string: str) -> List[int]:
    result = []
    for group in paren_string.split():
        max_depth = 0
        current_depth = 0
        for char in group:
            if char == '(':
                current_depth += 1
                max_depth = max(max_depth, current_depth)
            elif char == ')':
                current_depth -= 1
        result.append(max_depth)
    return result
```

```python
from typing import List

def filter_by_substring(strings: List[str], substring: str) -> List[str]:
    filtered_strings = []
    for string in strings:
        if substring in string:
            filtered_strings.append(string)
    return filtered_strings
```

### Code
```python
from typing import List, Tuple

def sum_product(numbers: List[int]) -> Tuple[int, int]:
    sum_result = 0
    product_result = 1
    for num in numbers:
        sum_result += num
        product_result *= num
    return sum_result, product_result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the rolling maximum elements.
2. Iterate through the input list of numbers.
3. At each iteration, calculate the maximum element from the current element and all previous elements seen so far.
4. Append this maximum element to the rolling maximum list.
5. Return the list of rolling maximum elements.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def rolling_max(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    rolling_max_list = []
    max_num = float('-inf')
    
    for num in numbers:
        max_num = max(max_num, num)
        rolling_max_list.append(max_num)
    
    return rolling_max_list
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the shortest palindrome that begins with the supplied string, we need to follow these steps:
    - Find the longest postfix of the supplied string that is a palindrome.
    - Append to the end of the string the reverse of the string prefix that comes before the palindromic suffix.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_palindrome(string: str) -> bool:
    """ Test if given string is a palindrome """
    return string == string[::-1]


def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string """
    if not string:
        return ''
    
    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[:i-1][::-1]
    
    return string + string[:-1][::-1]
```

```python
def is_palindrome(string: str) -> bool:
    """ Test if given string is a palindrome """
    return string == string[::-1]

def make_palindrome(string: str) -> str:
    """ Find the shortest palindrome that begins with a supplied string """
    if not string:
        return ''

    for i in range(len(string), 0, -1):
        if is_palindrome(string[:i]):
            return string + string[i-1::-1]  # Fix the index to correctly append the reverse prefix
    
    return string + string[:-1][::-1]
```

```
def string_xor(a: str, b: str) -> str:
    result = ""
    for i in range(len(a)):
        if a[i] == b[i]:
            result += '0'
        else:
            result += '1'
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input list is empty. If it is empty, return None.
2. Iterate over the list of strings and keep track of the longest string seen so far.
3. If we encounter a string that is longer than the current longest string, update the longest string.
4. If we encounter a string of the same length as the current longest string, keep the current longest string (as we need to return the first one in case of multiple strings of the same length).
5. After iterating over all strings, return the longest string found.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List, Optional

def longest(strings: List[str]) -> Optional[str]:
    if not strings:
        return None

    longest_str = strings[0]
    for s in strings[1:]:
        if len(s) > len(longest_str):
            longest_str = s
    return longest_str
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the greatest common divisor (GCD) of two integers `a` and `b`.
2. We can use the Euclidean algorithm to find the GCD of two numbers.
3. The algorithm states that the GCD of two numbers `a` and `b` is the same as the GCD of `b` and `a % b`.
4. We apply this recursively until `b` becomes 0, at which point `a` will be the GCD.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def greatest_common_divisor(a: int, b: int) -> int:
    while b != 0:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a
```

```python
def count_distinct_characters(string: str) -> int:

    string = string.lower()
    # Use a set to store unique characters
    distinct_chars = set(string)
    # Return the count of distinct characters
    return len(distinct_chars)
```

```python
from typing import List

def parse_music(music_string: str) -> List[int]:
    note_duration = {'o': 4, 'o|': 2, '.|': 1}
    result = []
    
    for note in music_string.split():
        result.append(note_duration.get(note))
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find how many times a given substring can be found in the original string.
2. The function should count overlapping cases, meaning if the substring appears multiple times in the original string with overlaps, each occurrence should be counted.
3. We can achieve this by iterating over the original string and checking if the substring matches the corresponding slice of the original string.
4. We increment a counter whenever a match is found.
5. Finally, we return the counter as the result.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def how_many_times(string: str, substring: str) -> int:
    count = 0
    if not string or not substring:
        return 0
    
    for i in range(len(string) - len(substring) + 1):
        if string[i:i + len(substring)] == substring:
            count += 1
    
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map each word numeral to its integer equivalent.
2. Split the input `numbers` string by spaces to get a list of word numerals.
3. Convert each word numeral to its corresponding integer using the dictionary from step 1.
4. Sort the list of integers in ascending order.
5. Map the sorted integers back to their word numeral representations.
6. Join the sorted word numerals with spaces to form the final sorted string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def sort_numbers(numbers: str) -> str:
    # Dictionary to map word numerals to integers
    numeral_to_int = {
        'zero': 0,
        'one': 1,
        'two': 2,
        'three': 3,
        'four': 4,
        'five': 5,
        'six': 6,
        'seven': 7,
        'eight': 8,
        'nine': 9
    }
    
    # Split the input string by spaces
    numerals = numbers.split()
    
    # Convert word numerals to integers and sort
    sorted_numerals = sorted([numeral_to_int[num] for num in numerals])
    
    # Map sorted integers back to word numerals
    sorted_words = [key for value, key in sorted([(numeral_to_int[num], num) for num in numerals])]
    
    # Join sorted word numerals with spaces
    return ' '.join(sorted_words)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the two closest elements in the list of numbers, we can start by sorting the list in ascending order.
2. Then, we can iterate through the sorted list and calculate the difference between each adjacent pair of numbers.
3. Keep track of the pair with the smallest difference.
4. Finally, return this pair in ascending order.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List, Tuple

def find_closest_elements(numbers: List[float]) -> Tuple[float, float]:
    numbers.sort()  # Sort the list in ascending order
    min_diff = float('inf')  # Initialize minimum difference to positive infinity
    result = ()
    
    for i in range(len(numbers) - 1):
        diff = abs(numbers[i] - numbers[i+1])  # Calculate the difference between adjacent numbers
        if diff < min_diff:
            min_diff = diff
            result = (numbers[i], numbers[i+1])
    
    return result
```

3. For each number in the list, calculate the rescaled value using the formula: `(number - minimum) / range`.
4. Append the rescaled values to a new list and return the new list.

### Requirements

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def rescale_to_unit(numbers: List[float]) -> List[float]:
    min_num = min(numbers)
    max_num = max(numbers)
    range_num = max_num - min_num
    
    rescaled_numbers = [(num - min_num) / range_num for num in numbers]
    
    return rescaled_numbers
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the largest number that divides the given number `n` evenly, smaller than `n`.
2. We can start by initializing a variable `largest_div` to store the largest divisor found so far. We can start with `largest_div = 1`.
3. We will iterate from `2` up to `n//2` because the largest divisor of `n` smaller than `n` will always be less than `n//2`.
4. For each number `i` in the range, we will check if `n` is divisible by `i`. If it is divisible, we update `largest_div` to `i`.
5. Finally, we return the `largest_div`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def largest_divisor(n: int) -> int:
    largest_div = 1
    for i in range(2, n//2 + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            largest_div = i
    return largest_div
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the prime factors.
2. Start dividing the input number `n` by 2 until it is divisible by 2. Add 2 to the list of prime factors each time it is divided by 2.
3. After dividing by 2, start dividing by 3 until it is divisible by 3. Add 3 to the list of prime factors each time it is divided by 3.
4. Continue this process for increasing odd integers until `n` becomes 1.
5. Return the list of prime factors in the order from smallest to largest.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def factorize(n: int) -> List[int]:
    factors = []
    divisor = 2
    while n > 1:
        if n % divisor == 0:
            factors.append(divisor)
            n //= divisor
        else:
            divisor += 1
    return factors
```

2. To achieve this, we can iterate through the list and keep track of seen elements using a set.
3. If an element is not in the set, we add it to the result list and the set.

### Requirements

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def remove_duplicates(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    seen = set()
    result = []
    
    for num in numbers:
        if num not in seen:
            result.append(num)
            seen.add(num)
    
    return result
```

```python
from typing import List

def remove_duplicates(numbers: List[int]) -> List[int]:
    seen = set()

    for num in numbers:
```

```python
def flip_case(string: str) -> str:
    flipped_string = ""
    for char in string:
        if char.islower():
            flipped_string += char.upper()
        elif char.isupper():
            flipped_string += char.lower()
        else:
            flipped_string += char
    return flipped_string
```

### Code
```python
from typing import List

def filter_by_prefix(strings: List[str], prefix: str) -> List[str]:
    filtered_strings = []
    for string in strings:
        if string.startswith(prefix):
            filtered_strings.append(string)
    return filtered_strings
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input number `n` is less than 2. If it is, return False because prime numbers start from 2.
2. Loop from 2 to the square root of `n` (inclusive). Check if `n` is divisible by any number in this range.
3. If `n` is divisible by any number in the range, return False because it is not a prime number.
4. If the loop completes without finding any divisors, return True because `n` is a prime number.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
import math

def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            return False
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The `find_zero` function aims to find a zero point of a polynomial function defined by the coefficients provided in the input list `xs`.
2. The function should check if the length of the coefficients list is even, as it only works for polynomials with an even number of coefficients.
3. Next, it should find the largest non-zero coefficient in the list to guarantee a solution.
4. Once the largest non-zero coefficient is identified, the function should construct a new list of coefficients for a shifted polynomial to find the zero point.
5. The shifted polynomial can be constructed by dividing all coefficients by the largest non-zero coefficient.
6. After constructing the shifted polynomial, the function should use the `poly` function to find the zero point of the shifted polynomial.
7. Finally, the zero point found for the shifted polynomial needs to be adjusted back to the original polynomial using the same scaling factor.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def poly(xs: list, x: float):
    """
    Evaluates polynomial with coefficients xs at point x.
    return xs[0] + xs[1] * x + xs[1] * x^2 + .... xs[n] * x^n
    """
    return sum([coeff * math.pow(x, i) for i, coeff in enumerate(xs)])


def find_zero(xs: list):
    """ xs are coefficients of a polynomial.
    find_zero find x such that poly(x) = 0.
    find_zero returns only only zero point, even if there are many.
    Moreover, find_zero only takes list xs having even number of coefficients
    and largest non zero coefficient as it guarantees
    a solution.
    >>> round(find_zero([1, 2]), 2) # f(x) = 1 + 2x
    -0.5
    >>> round(find_zero([-6, 11, -6, 1]), 2) # (x - 1) * (x - 2) * (x - 3) = -6 + 11x - 6x^2 + x^3
    1.0
    """
    if len(xs) % 2 != 0:
        raise ValueError("Number of coefficients should be even.")
    
    largest_non_zero_coeff = max([abs(coeff) for coeff in xs if coeff != 0])
    
    shifted_xs = [coeff / largest_non_zero_coeff for coeff in xs]
    
    zero_point_shifted = poly(shifted_xs, 0)
    
    return zero_point_shifted * largest_non_zero_coeff
```

```python
import math

def poly(coeffs: list, x: float):
    """
    Evaluates polynomial with coefficients coeffs at point x.
    return coeffs[0] + coeffs[1] * x + coeffs[2] * x^2 + .... coeffs[n] * x^n
    """
    return sum([coeff * math.pow(x, i) for i, coeff in enumerate(coeffs)])

def find_zero(coeffs: list):
    """ coeffs are coefficients of a polynomial.
    find_zero find x such that poly(x) = 0.
    find_zero returns only only zero point, even if there are many.
    Moreover, find_zero only takes list coeffs having even number of coefficients
    and largest non zero coefficient as it guarantees
    a solution.
    """
    if len(coeffs) % 2 != 0:
        raise ValueError("Number of coefficients should be even.")
    
    largest_non_zero_coeff = max([abs(coeff) for coeff in coeffs if coeff != 0])
    
    shifted_coeffs = [coeff / largest_non_zero_coeff for coeff in coeffs]
    
    zero_point_shifted = poly(shifted_coeffs, 0)
```

### Code
```python
def sort_third(l: list):
    result = []
    for i in range(len(l)):
        if i % 3 == 0:
            result.extend(sorted(l[i:i+3]))
        else:
            result.append(l[i])
    return result
```

```python
def sort_third(l: list):
    result = []
    for i in range(len(l)):
```
```python
        if (i + 1) % 3 == 0:  
            result.extend(sorted(l[max(0, i-2):i+1]))  
        else:
            result.append(l[i])
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the number of times digit 7 appears.
2. Iterate over integers from 1 to n-1.
3. Check if the integer is divisible by either 11 or 13.
4. If the integer is divisible by 11 or 13, convert the integer to a string and count the occurrences of digit '7'.
5. Increment the `count` variable by the number of occurrences of digit '7'.
6. Return the final `count` value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def fizz_buzz(n: int):
    count = 0
    for i in range(1, n):
        if i % 11 == 0 or i % 13 == 0:
            count += str(i).count('7')
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To decode the cyclic encoded string, we need to reverse the encoding process performed by the `encode_cyclic` function.
2. In the `encode_cyclic` function, the input string is split into groups of three characters and each group is cyclically shifted by one position to the left.
3. To decode the cyclic encoded string, we need to reverse this process by shifting each group of three characters by one position to the right.
4. If the last group has fewer than three characters, we need to handle it separately.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def encode_cyclic(s: str):
    """
    returns encoded string by cycling groups of three characters.
    """
    groups = [s[(3 * i):min((3 * i + 3), len(s))] for i in range((len(s) + 2) // 3)]
    groups = [(group[1:] + group[0]) if len(group) == 3 else group for group in groups]
    return "".join(groups)


def decode_cyclic(s: str):
    """
    takes as input string encoded with encode_cyclic function. Returns decoded string.
    """
    # split string to groups. Each of length 3.
    groups = [s[(3 * i):min((3 * i + 3), len(s))] for i in range((len(s) + 2) // 3)]
    # reverse cycle elements in each group. Unless group has fewer elements than 3.
    groups = [(group[-1] + group[:-1]) if len(group) == 3 else group for group in groups]
    return "".join(groups)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to generate Fibonacci numbers until we find the n-th number that is both a Fibonacci number and a prime number.
2. To generate Fibonacci numbers, we can start with the first two numbers in the sequence (0 and 1) and then keep adding the previous two numbers to get the next number in the sequence.
3. We need a function to check if a number is prime. A prime number is a number greater than 1 that is only divisible by 1 and itself.
4. We will iterate through Fibonacci numbers and check if each number is prime until we find the n-th number that satisfies both conditions.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num <= 1:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def prime_fib(n: int):
    if n <= 0:
        return "Invalid input. n should be a positive integer."

    fib_sequence = [0, 1]
    count = 2
    while count < n:
        next_num = fib_sequence[-1] + fib_sequence[-2]
        if is_prime(next_num):
            count += 1
        fib_sequence.append(next_num)

    return fib_sequence[-1]

# Test cases
print(prime_fib(1))  # Output: 2
print(prime_fib(2))  # Output: 3
print(prime_fib(3))  # Output: 5
print(prime_fib(4))  # Output: 13
print(prime_fib(5))  # Output: 89
```

5. The function checks for prime numbers using the `is_prime` function.
6. If the input `n` is less than or equal to 0, the function returns a message "Invalid input. n should be a positive integer."
7. The Fibonacci sequence is generated iteratively until the nth Fibonacci prime number is found.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num <= 1:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def prime_fib(n: int):
    if n <= 0:
        return "Invalid input. n should be a positive integer."

    fib_sequence = [0, 1]
    count = 2
    while count < n:
        next_num = fib_sequence[-1] + fib_sequence[-2]
        if is_prime(next_num):
            count += 1
        fib_sequence.append(next_num)

    return fib_sequence[-1]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find three distinct elements in the input list that sum to zero.
2. To solve this problem, we will use a nested loop to iterate through all possible combinations of three elements from the input list.
3. For each combination, we will check if the sum of the three elements is zero.
4. If we find any such combination, we will return True, else we will return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def triples_sum_to_zero(l: list):
    if len(l) < 3:
        return False
    
    for i in range(len(l)-2):
        for j in range(i+1, len(l)-1):
            for k in range(j+1, len(l)):
                if l[i] + l[j] + l[k] == 0:
                    return True
    
    return False
```

```
def car_race_collision(n: int):
    """
    Imagine a road that's a perfectly straight infinitely long line.
    n cars are driving left to right; simultaneously, a different set of n cars
    are driving right to left. The two sets of cars start out being very far from
    each other. All cars move in the same speed. Two cars are said to collide
    when a car that's moving left to right hits a car that's moving right to left.
    However, the cars are infinitely sturdy and strong; as a result, they continue moving
    in their trajectory as if they did not collide.

    This function outputs the number of such collisions.
    """
    return n * (n - 1) // 2
```

### Requirements

### Code
```
def pairs_sum_to_zero(l):
    seen = set()
    for num in l:
        if -num in seen:
            return True
        seen.add(num)
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function named `change_base` that takes two parameters `x` and `base`.
2. Convert the integer input number `x` to the desired base using the given `base`.
3. Return the string representation of the converted number.
4. Make sure the base is less than 10 as per the requirement.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def change_base(x: int, base: int):
    if base < 2 or base > 9:
        raise ValueError("Base should be between 2 and 9")
    
    if x == 0:
        return '0'
    
    result = ''
    while x > 0:
        remainder = x % base
        result = str(remainder) + result
        x = x // base
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to create a function to efficiently compute the n-th element of the Fib4 number sequence.
2. We cannot use recursion, so we need to use an iterative approach.
3. We will initialize a list to store the first four elements of the sequence.
4. Then we will iterate from 4 to n and calculate each element of the sequence using the formula mentioned in the description.
5. At the end, we will return the n-th element of the Fib4 number sequence.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def fib4(n: int):
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    if n == 1:
        return 0
    if n == 2:
        return 2
    if n == 3:
        return 0
    
    sequence = [0, 0, 2, 0]
    
    for i in range(4, n+1):
        fib_n = sequence[-1] + sequence[-2] + sequence[-3] + sequence[-4]
        sequence.append(fib_n)
    
    return sequence[-1]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Sort the input list `l` in ascending order.
2. Check if the length of the list is odd or even.
3. If the length is odd, return the middle element.
4. If the length is even, return the average of the two middle elements.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def median(l: list):
    l_sorted = sorted(l)
    n = len(l_sorted)
    if n % 2 == 1:
        return l_sorted[n // 2]
    else:
        return (l_sorted[n // 2 - 1] + l_sorted[n // 2]) / 2
```

### Requirements

### Code
```python
def is_palindrome(text: str):
    # Convert the text to lowercase for case-insensitive comparison
    text = text.lower()
    # Check if the text is equal to its reverse
    return text == text[::-1]
```

3. To handle the wrap-around when subtracting 5, we can use the modulus operator `%` to ensure the result falls within the range of lowercase letters (97 to 122 in ASCII).

### Code
```
def decode_shift(s: str):
    return "".join([chr(((ord(ch) - 5 - ord("a")) % 26) + ord("a")) for ch in s])
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The Fibonacci sequence is a series of numbers where each number is the sum of the two preceding ones.
2. We can solve this problem using recursion or iteration.
3. We will start by checking if the input `n` is less than or equal to 1. If so, we return `n`.
4. If `n` is greater than 1, we will calculate the Fibonacci number using a loop or recursion until we reach the `n`-th Fibonacci number.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def fib(n: int):
    if n <= 1:
        return n
    else:
        a, b = 0, 1
        for _ in range(2, n+1):
            a, b = b, a + b
        return b
```

```python
    opening_count = 0
    closing_count = 0
    
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == "<":
            opening_count += 1
        elif bracket == ">":
            closing_count += 1
    
    return opening_count == closing_count
```

### Code
```python
def correct_bracketing(brackets: str):
    stack = []
    
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == "<":
            stack.append(bracket)
        elif bracket == ">":
            if not stack:
                return False
            else:
                stack.pop()
    
    return len(stack) == 0
```

2. To do this, we can iterate through the list and compare each element with the next one.
3. If all elements are either increasing or decreasing, then the list is monotonic.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def monotonic(l: list):
    increasing = all(l[i] <= l[i + 1] for i in range(len(l) - 1))
    decreasing = all(l[i] >= l[i + 1] for i in range(len(l) - 1))
    return increasing or decreasing
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the common elements between two lists.
2. First, we will convert the lists into sets to get unique elements from each list.
3. Then, we will find the intersection of the two sets to get the common elements.
4. Finally, we will return the sorted common elements as a list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def common(l1: list, l2: list):
    set1 = set(l1)
    set2 = set(l2)
    
    common_elements = list(set1.intersection(set2))
    common_elements.sort()
    
    return common_elements
```

1. Iterate through numbers starting from 2 (smallest prime number) up to the square root of n.
2. For each number, check if it is a factor of n.
3. If a number is a factor of n, check if it is a prime number.
4. Keep track of the largest prime factor found.
5. Return the largest prime factor.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def largest_prime_factor(n: int):
    largest_prime = 0
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            if is_prime(i):
                largest_prime = i
    return largest_prime

# Test cases
# print(largest_prime_factor(13195))  # Output: 29
# print(largest_prime_factor(2048))   # Output: 2
```

```
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def candidate(n: int):
    largest_prime = 0
    while n % 2 == 0:
        largest_prime = 2
        n = n // 2
    for i in range(3, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1, 2):
        while n % i == 0:
            largest_prime = i
            n = n // i
    if n > 2:
        return n
    return largest_prime

def largest_prime_factor(n: int):
    largest_prime = candidate(n)
    return largest_prime
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if every opening bracket has a corresponding closing bracket in the given string of brackets.
2. We can achieve this by iterating through each character in the string.
3. We will use a stack to keep track of opening brackets.
4. If we encounter an opening bracket, we push it onto the stack.
5. If we encounter a closing bracket, we check if the stack is empty (meaning no opening bracket for the closing bracket) or the top of the stack is not an opening bracket. In this case, we return False.
6. If all brackets are correctly paired, we return True at the end.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def correct_bracketing(brackets: str):
    stack = []
    for bracket in brackets:
        if bracket == '(':
            stack.append(bracket)
        elif bracket == ')':
            if not stack or stack[-1] != '(':
                return False
            stack.pop()
    return len(stack) == 0
```

1. The derivative of a polynomial term ax^n is n * ax^(n-1).
2. Iterate through the list of coefficients, skipping the first element (constant term) as it does not affect the derivative.
3. For each coefficient at index i and value a, multiply it by i to get the derivative of this term.

5. Return the list of derivative coefficients.

### Requirements

### Code
```
def derivative(xs: list):
    derivative_coeffs = []
    for i in range(1, len(xs)):
        derivative_coeffs.append(xs[i] * i)
    return derivative_coeffs
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input `n` is less than or equal to 2. If so, return 0 for `n=0` and 1 for `n=2`.
2. Create a list to store the FibFib sequence values.
3. Initialize the list with values `[0, 0, 1]` to cover the base cases.
4. Iterate starting from index 3 up to `n` and calculate the FibFib value at each index using the formula `fibfib(n) = fibfib(n-1) + fibfib(n-2) + fibfib(n-3)`.
5. Return the FibFib value at index `n`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def fibfib(n: int):
    if n <= 2:
        return [0, 0, 1][n]
    
    fibfib_sequence = [0, 0, 1]
    for i in range(3, n+1):
        fibfib_n = fibfib_sequence[i-1] + fibfib_sequence[i-2] + fibfib_sequence[i-3]
        fibfib_sequence.append(fibfib_n)
    
    return fibfib_sequence[n]
```

```
def vowels_count(s):
    vowels = ['a', 'e', 'i', 'o', 'u']
    count = 0
    for i in range(len(s)):
        if s[i].lower() in vowels:
            count += 1
        elif i == len(s) - 1 and s[i].lower() == 'y':
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the integer x to a string to easily manipulate the digits.
2. Determine the number of digits in x.
3. If shift is greater than the number of digits, reverse the string and return the result.
4. Otherwise, perform a circular shift by shifting the digits to the right by shift.
5. Return the result as a string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def circular_shift(x, shift):
    x_str = str(x)
    num_digits = len(x_str)
    
    if shift > num_digits:
        return x_str[::-1]
    
    shift %= num_digits
    shifted_str = x_str[-shift:] + x_str[:-shift]
    
    return shifted_str
```

1. Initialize a variable `total_sum` to keep track of the sum of ASCII codes of upper characters.
2. Iterate through each character in the input string `s`.
3. Check if the character is an uppercase character using the `isupper()` method.
4. If the character is uppercase, add its ASCII value to the `total_sum`.
5. Finally, return the `total_sum`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def digitSum(s):
    total_sum = 0
    for char in s:
        if char.isupper():
            total_sum += ord(char)
    return total_sum
```

1. Split the input string to extract the number of apples and oranges.

### Requirements

### Code
```python
def fruit_distribution(s, n):
    # Split the string to extract the number of apples and oranges
    fruits = s.split()
    apples = int(fruits[0])
    oranges = int(fruits[3])
    
    # Calculate the total number of mango fruits
    mango = n - apples - oranges
    
    return mango
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize variables to keep track of the smallest even value and its index.
2. Iterate through the array and check if the current element is even and smaller than the current smallest even value.
3. If the condition is met, update the smallest even value and its index.
4. If there are multiple nodes with the same smallest even value, choose the one with the smallest index.
5. Return the smallest even value and its index in a list format.
6. If there are no even values or the array is empty, return an empty list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def pluck(arr):
    smallest_even = float('inf')
    smallest_even_index = -1
    
    for i, num in enumerate(arr):
        if num % 2 == 0 and num < smallest_even:
            smallest_even = num
            smallest_even_index = i
    
    if smallest_even_index == -1:
        return []
    
    return [smallest_even, smallest_even_index]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a dictionary to store the frequency of each integer in the input list.
2. Iterate through the input list and count the frequency of each integer by updating the dictionary.
3. Iterate over the dictionary items and find the greatest integer that satisfies the condition of having a frequency greater than or equal to the value of the integer itself.
4. If such an integer is found, return it. If no such integer is found, return -1.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def search(lst):
    frequency_map = {}
    
    # Count the frequency of each integer in the input list
    for num in lst:
        if num in frequency_map:
            frequency_map[num] += 1
        else:
            frequency_map[num] = 1
    
    max_num = -1
    for num, freq in frequency_map.items():
        if num > 0 and freq >= num:
            max_num = max(max_num, num)
    
    return max_num
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. First, we need to sort the given list of integers in ascending order.
2. Then, we can create a new list where we iterate through the sorted list and alternatively append the minimum and maximum values from the sorted list to the new list.
3. Finally, return the new list as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def strange_sort_list(lst):
    if not lst:
        return []
    
    sorted_lst = sorted(lst)
    result = []
    
    min_idx, max_idx = 0, len(sorted_lst) - 1
    
    while min_idx <= max_idx:
        result.append(sorted_lst[min_idx])
        if min_idx != max_idx:
            result.append(sorted_lst[max_idx])
        min_idx += 1
        max_idx -= 1
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the given three sides can form a valid triangle by verifying whether the sum of any two sides is greater than the third side.
2. If the sides form a valid triangle, calculate the area using Heron's formula.
3. Heron's formula to calculate the area of a triangle given its three sides: 
   - Let `a`, `b`, and `c` be the three sides of the triangle.
   - Calculate the semi-perimeter, `s`, using the formula: `s = (a + b + c) / 2`
   - Calculate the area using the formula: `Area = √(s * (s - a) * (s - b) * (s - c))`.
4. Round the calculated area to 2 decimal points.
5. If the sides do not form a valid triangle, return -1.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def triangle_area(a, b, c):
    # Check if the three sides form a valid triangle
    if a + b > c and a + c > b and b + c > a:
        # Calculate the semi-perimeter
        s = (a + b + c) / 2
        # Calculate the area using Heron's formula
        area = math.sqrt(s * (s - a) * (s - b) * (s - c))
        # Round the area to 2 decimal points
        return round(area, 2)
    else:
        return -1
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the object `q` is balanced by verifying if it is a palindromic list. A list is palindromic if it reads the same forwards and backwards.
2. Calculate the sum of all elements in the list `q`.
3. Check if the sum of elements in `q` is less than or equal to the maximum possible weight `w`.
4. If both conditions are satisfied, return True indicating that the object will fly, otherwise return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def will_it_fly(q, w):
    # Check if the list is palindromic
    if q == q[::-1]:
        # Calculate the sum of elements in the list
        total_weight = sum(q)
        # Check if the sum of elements is less than or equal to the maximum weight
        if total_weight <= w:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To find the minimum number of elements that need to be changed to make the array palindromic, we can follow these steps:
2. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the number of changes needed.
3. Loop through the array from the start and end simultaneously until they meet in the middle.
4. For each pair of elements at the corresponding positions, if they are not equal, increment `count` by 1.
5. The final `count` will be the minimum number of changes needed to make the array palindromic.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def smallest_change(arr):
    count = 0
    n = len(arr)
    for i in range(n // 2):
        if arr[i] != arr[n - 1 - i]:
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to compare the total number of characters in all strings of two given lists.
2. If the total number of characters in the first list is less than the total number of characters in the second list, return the first list.
3. If the total number of characters in the second list is less than the total number of characters in the first list, return the second list.
4. If the total number of characters in both lists are equal, return the first list.
5. To calculate the total number of characters in a list, we sum the lengths of all strings in that list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def total_match(lst1, lst2):
    total_chars_lst1 = sum(len(s) for s in lst1)
    total_chars_lst2 = sum(len(s) for s in lst2)
    
    if total_chars_lst1 < total_chars_lst2:
        return lst1
    elif total_chars_lst2 < total_chars_lst1:
        return lst2
    else:
        return lst1
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if the given number `a` is the multiplication of 3 prime numbers.
2. We can start by writing a helper function to check if a number is prime.
3. Then, we can iterate through all possible combinations of 3 prime numbers that multiply to `a`.
4. If we find such a combination, we return True. Otherwise, we return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def is_multiply_prime(a):
    for i in range(2, a):
        if is_prime(i) and a % i == 0:
            for j in range(i + 1, a):
                if is_prime(j) and a % (i * j) == 0:
                    k = a // (i * j)
                    if is_prime(k):
                        return True
    return False

# Test the function
print(is_multiply_prime(30))  # Output should be True
```

```python
import itertools

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def is_multiply_prime(a):
    primes = [i for i in range(2, 100) if is_prime(i)]
    for combination in itertools.combinations(primes, 3):
        if combination[0] * combination[1] * combination[2] == a:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To determine if a number `x` is a simple power of another number `n`, we need to check if `n` raised to an integer power results in `x`.
2. We can iterate through integer powers starting from 1 until the result exceeds or equals `x`.
3. If at any point the result equals `x`, then `x` is a simple power of `n` and we return `True`. If we exceed `x` without finding a match, then we return `False`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_simple_power(x, n):
    power = 1
    result = n
    while result < x:
        power += 1
        result = n ** power
    return result == x
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function `is_prime` to check if a number is prime by iterating from 2 to the square root of the number and checking if the number is divisible by any number in that range.
2. Define a function `hex_key` that takes a hexadecimal number as input.
3. Create a variable `prime_count` to keep track of the count of prime hexadecimal digits.
4. Iterate through each character in the input hexadecimal number.
5. For each character:
    - Convert the character to its corresponding decimal value.
    - Check if the decimal value is in the set of prime numbers {2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13}.
    - If it is a prime number, increment the `prime_count`.
6. Return the `prime_count`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
import math

def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(num)) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def hex_key(num):
    prime_digits = {2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13}
    prime_count = 0
    for char in num:
        decimal_value = int(char, 16)
        if decimal_value in prime_digits and is_prime(decimal_value):
            prime_count += 1
    return prime_count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To convert a decimal number to binary, we can use the built-in `bin()` function in Python.
2. The `bin()` function returns a string representing the binary form of the given decimal number. It includes the prefix '0b' to indicate that the result is in binary format.
3. We need to remove the '0b' prefix and add the 'db' characters at the beginning and end of the binary string before returning it.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def decimal_to_binary(decimal):
    binary = bin(decimal)[2:]  # Get the binary representation without the '0b' prefix
    return 'db' + binary + 'db'  # Add the 'db' characters at the beginning and end
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the length of the string is less than 3, return `False` as per the condition.
2. Iterate through the string up to the 3rd last character.
3. For each character, check if it is equal to the next two characters. If any three consecutive characters are the same, return `False`.
4. If the loop completes without finding any three consecutive characters that are the same, return `True`.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_happy(s):
    if len(s) < 3:
        return False
    
    for i in range(len(s) - 2):
        if s[i] == s[i+1] or s[i] == s[i+2] or s[i+1] == s[i+2]:
            return False
    
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a function `numerical_letter_grade` that takes a list of GPAs as input.
2. Iterate through the list of GPAs.
3. For each GPA, determine the corresponding letter grade based on the provided grading table.
4. Append the letter grade to a new list.
5. Return the list of letter grades.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def numerical_letter_grade(grades):
    letter_grades = []
    for gpa in grades:
        if gpa == 4.0:
            letter_grades.append('A+')
        elif gpa > 3.7:
            letter_grades.append('A')
        elif gpa > 3.3:
            letter_grades.append('A-')
        elif gpa > 3.0:
            letter_grades.append('B+')
        elif gpa > 2.7:
            letter_grades.append('B')
        elif gpa > 2.3:
            letter_grades.append('B-')
        elif gpa > 2.0:
            letter_grades.append('C+')
        elif gpa > 1.7:
            letter_grades.append('C')
        elif gpa > 1.3:
            letter_grades.append('C-')
        elif gpa > 1.0:
            letter_grades.append('D+')
        elif gpa > 0.7:
            letter_grades.append('D')
        else:
            letter_grades.append('E')
    return letter_grades
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Calculate the length of the input string.
2. Check if the length is a prime number.
3. Return True if the length is a prime number, otherwise return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def prime_length(string):
    length = len(string)
    return is_prime(length)

# Test cases
# print(prime_length('Hello'))  # True
# print(prime_length('abcdcba'))  # True
# print(prime_length('kittens'))  # True
# print(prime_length('orange'))  # False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the count of n-digit positive integers that start or end with 1.
2. Iterate through all n-digit positive integers.
3. For each integer, check if it starts with 1 or ends with 1.
4. If it starts or ends with 1, increment the `count`.
5. Return the final `count` as the result.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def starts_one_ends(n):
    count = 0
    for i in range(10**(n-1), 10**n):
        if str(i)[0] == '1' or str(i)[-1] == '1':
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the given positive integer `N` into its binary representation.
2. Calculate the sum of digits in the binary representation.
3. Return the sum of digits as a binary string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def solve(N):
    # Convert the integer N to binary and remove the '0b' prefix
    binary = bin(N)[2:]
    
    # Calculate the sum of digits in the binary representation
    sum_of_digits = sum(int(digit) for digit in binary)
    
    # Return the sum of digits as a binary string
    return bin(sum_of_digits)[2:]
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `total` to 0 to keep track of the sum of even elements at odd indices.
2. Iterate over the list `lst` starting from index 1 (odd indices).
3. Check if the element at the current index is even.
4. If the element is even, add it to `total`.
5. Return the value of `total` as the result.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def add(lst):
    total = 0
    for i in range(1, len(lst), 2):
        if lst[i] % 2 == 0:
            total += lst[i]
    return total
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string into individual words.
2. For each word, sort the characters in ascending order based on their ASCII values.
3. Join the sorted words back together with spaces.
4. Return the final ordered string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def anti_shuffle(s):
    words = s.split()
    ordered_words = [''.join(sorted(word)) for word in words]
    ordered_string = ' '.join(ordered_words)
    return ordered_string
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through each row in the 2-dimensional list.
2. For each row, check if the integer `x` is present in that row.
3. If `x` is present in the row, store the coordinates (row index, column index of `x`) in a list of tuples.
4. Sort the list of tuples by rows in ascending order.
5. Sort the list of tuples by columns in descending order within each row.
6. Return the sorted list of tuples.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def get_row(lst, x):
    coordinates = []
    
    for i, row in enumerate(lst):
        for j, value in enumerate(row):
            if value == x:
                coordinates.append((i, j))
    
    coordinates.sort(key=lambda coord: (coord[0], -coord[1]))
    
    return coordinates
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the sum of the first and last elements of the array is odd or even.
2. If the sum is odd, create a sorted copy of the array in ascending order.
3. If the sum is even, create a sorted copy of the array in descending order.
4. Return the sorted copy without modifying the original array.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def sort_array(array):
    if not array:
        return array
    
    first_value = array[0]
    last_value = array[-1]
    
    if (first_value + last_value) % 2 == 1:  # sum is odd
        return sorted(array)
    else:  # sum is even
        return sorted(array, reverse=True)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map the original alphabet to the rotated alphabet where each letter is shifted down by two multiplied by two places.
2. Iterate through each character in the input string and check if it is an alphabet character.
3. If the character is an alphabet character, find its corresponding rotated character from the dictionary and append it to the result string.
4. If the character is not an alphabet character, simply append it as it is to the result string.
5. Return the final encrypted string.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def encrypt(s):
    original_alphabet = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
    rotated_alphabet = 'cdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyzab'
    
    result = ''
    for char in s:
        if char.isalpha():
            index = original_alphabet.index(char.lower())
            rotated_char = rotated_alphabet[index]
            if char.isupper():
                rotated_char = rotated_char.upper()
            result += rotated_char
        else:
            result += char
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if all the inputs are integers.
2. If all inputs are integers, check if any one number is equal to the sum of the other two.
3. Return True if the condition is satisfied, otherwise return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def any_int(x, y, z):
    if all(isinstance(num, int) for num in [x, y, z]):
        if x == y + z or y == x + z or z == x + y:
            return True
    return False
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create a dictionary to map vowels to the letter that appears 2 places ahead of them in the English alphabet.
2. Iterate through each character in the message.
3. If the character is a letter, swap its case.
4. If the character is a vowel, replace it with the corresponding letter from the dictionary.
5. Return the modified message.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def encode(message):
    vowels = {'a': 'c', 'e': 'g', 'i': 'k', 'o': 'q', 'u': 'w', 'A': 'C', 'E': 'G', 'I': 'K', 'O': 'Q', 'U': 'W'}
    encoded_message = ''
    for char in message:
        if char.isalpha():
            if char.lower() in vowels:
                encoded_message += vowels[char.lower()]
            else:
                encoded_message += char.swapcase()
        else:
            encoded_message += char
    return encoded_message
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a helper function `is_prime(num)` to check if a number is prime or not.
2. Iterate through the given list of integers.
3. For each integer, check if it is prime using the `is_prime` helper function.
4. If the integer is prime, find the sum of its digits and keep track of the largest prime value found so far.
5. Finally, return the sum of digits of the largest prime value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def skjkasdkd(lst):
    def digit_sum(num):
        return sum(int(digit) for digit in str(num))

    largest_prime_sum = 0
    for num in lst:
        if is_prime(num):
            current_sum = digit_sum(num)
            if current_sum > largest_prime_sum:
                largest_prime_sum = current_sum
    
    return largest_prime_sum
```

```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def skjkasdkd(lst):
    def digit_sum(num):
        return sum(int(digit) for digit in str(num))

    largest_prime_sum = 0
    for num in lst:
        if is_prime(num):
            current_sum = digit_sum(num)
            if current_sum > largest_prime_sum:
                largest_prime_sum = current_sum
```

```python
def is_prime(num):
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num ** 0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def skjkasdkd(lst):
    def digit_sum(num):
        return sum(int(digit) for digit in str(num))

    largest_prime = float('-inf')  # Initialize with negative infinity to find the largest prime
    for num in lst:
        if is_prime(num):
            if num > largest_prime:
                largest_prime = num
    
    if largest_prime == float('-inf'):  # If no prime number found
        return 0
    else:
        return digit_sum(largest_prime)
```

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def check_dict_case(dict):
    if not dict:
        return False
    
    keys = dict.keys()
    
    if all(isinstance(key, str) and key.islower() for key in keys) or all(isinstance(key, str) and key.isupper() for key in keys):
        return True
    
    return False
```

```python
    if num < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def count_up_to(n):
    primes = []
    for i in range(2, n):
        if is_prime(i):
            primes.append(i)
    return primes
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Convert the input `value` which is a string representing a number to a float to handle decimal values.
2. Calculate the floor and ceiling of the float value.
3. Check which integer is closer to the original number.
4. If the number is equidistant from two integers, round it away from zero i.e., round it to the farthest integer from zero.
5. Return the closest integer.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def closest_integer(value):
    number = float(value)
    floor_value = int(number // 1)
    ceil_value = int(number // 1) + 1

    if abs(number - floor_value) < abs(number - ceil_value):
        return floor_value
    elif abs(number - floor_value) > abs(number - ceil_value):
        return ceil_value
    else:
        return ceil_value if number > 0 else floor_value
```

```python
def make_a_pile(n):
    stones = [n]
    for i in range(n-1):
        if stones[-1] % 2 == 0:
            stones.append(stones[-1] + 1)
        else:
            stones.append(stones[-1] + 2)
```

```python
def rounded_avg(n, m):
    if n > m:

    avg = round((n + m) / 2)
    return bin(avg)
```

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def unique_digits(x):
    def has_even_digit(num):
        for digit in str(num):
            if int(digit) % 2 == 0:
                return True
        return False

    result = [num for num in x if not has_even_digit(num)]
    return sorted(result)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Filter out the integers in the input array that are between 1 and 9 inclusive.
2. Sort the filtered integers.
3. Reverse the sorted array.
4. Replace each integer by its corresponding name from "One" to "Nine".
5. Return the resulting array.
6. Handle special cases where the input array is empty or contains strange numbers.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def by_length(arr):
    mapping = {
        1: "One",
        2: "Two",
        3: "Three",
        4: "Four",
        5: "Five",
        6: "Six",
        7: "Seven",
        8: "Eight",
        9: "Nine"
    }
    
    filtered_arr = [x for x in arr if 1 <= x <= 9]
    sorted_arr = sorted(filtered_arr)
    reversed_arr = sorted_arr[::-1]
    result = [mapping[num] for num in reversed_arr]
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Create an empty list to store the output.
2. Iterate over the range from 1 to n (inclusive).
3. For each index i:
   - If i is even, calculate the factorial of i.
   - If i is odd, calculate the sum of numbers from 1 to i.
4. Append the result to the output list.
5. Return the output list.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def f(n):
    def factorial(num):
        if num == 0:
            return 1
        else:
            return num * factorial(num - 1)

    result = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if i % 2 == 0:
            result.append(factorial(i))
        else:
            result.append(sum(range(1, i+1)))
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Define a function that takes a positive integer `n` as input.
2. Initialize two variables `even_count` and `odd_count` to count the number of even and odd palindromes.
3. Iterate over the range from 1 to `n` (inclusive).
4. For each number in the range, check if it is a palindrome.
5. If the number is a palindrome, increment the respective count based on whether it is even or odd.
6. Return a tuple containing the counts of even and odd palindromes.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def even_odd_palindrome(n):
    def is_palindrome(num):
        return str(num) == str(num)[::-1]
    
    even_count = 0
    odd_count = 0
    
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        if is_palindrome(i):
            if i % 2 == 0:
                even_count += 1
            else:
                odd_count += 1
    
    return (even_count, odd_count)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize a variable `count` to keep track of the number of elements with a sum of digits > 0.
2. Iterate through each element in the input array.
3. For each element, calculate the sum of its digits. If the sum is greater than 0, increment the `count` variable.
4. Handle the case of negative numbers by considering the first signed digit as negative if the number is negative.
5. Return the final count.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def count_nums(arr):
    count = 0
    for num in arr:
        sum_of_digits = sum(int(d) for d in str(abs(num)))
        if sum_of_digits > 0:
            count += 1
    return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the given array is empty. If it is empty, return True as the array is already sorted.
2. Find the index of the minimum element in the array.
3. Check if the array is already sorted in non-decreasing order. If it is sorted, return True.
4. Determine the number of right shifts required to move the minimum element to the beginning of the array.
5. Check if the array becomes sorted after performing the required number of right shifts. If it does, return True; otherwise, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def move_one_ball(arr):
    if not arr:
        return True
    
    min_index = arr.index(min(arr))
    if arr == sorted(arr):
        return True
    
    shifts_required = len(arr) - min_index
    new_arr = arr[-shifts_required:] + arr[:-shifts_required]
    
    return new_arr == sorted(arr)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to check if it is possible to exchange elements between `lst1` and `lst2` to make all elements in `lst1` even.
2. We will count the number of even elements in `lst1` and the total number of elements in `lst1`.
3. Calculate the number of odd elements in `lst1` by subtracting the number of even elements from the total number of elements.
4. If the number of odd elements in `lst1` is greater than the number of odd elements in `lst2`, it is not possible to exchange elements to make all elements in `lst1` even.
5. Otherwise, it is possible to exchange elements, return "YES".

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def exchange(lst1, lst2):
    even_count_lst1 = sum(1 for num in lst1 if num % 2 == 0)
    total_count_lst1 = len(lst1)
    odd_count_lst1 = total_count_lst1 - even_count_lst1
    
    odd_count_lst2 = sum(1 for num in lst2 if num % 2 != 0)
    
    if odd_count_lst1 > odd_count_lst2:
        return "NO"
    else:
        return "YES"
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string by space to get individual letters.
2. Create a dictionary to store the count of each letter.
3. Iterate through the letters and update the count in the dictionary.
4. Find the maximum count value in the dictionary.
5. Create a new dictionary containing only the letters with the maximum count value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def histogram(test):
    letters = test.split()
    letter_count = {}
    
    for letter in letters:
        if letter in letter_count:
            letter_count[letter] += 1
        else:
            letter_count[letter] = 1
            
    max_count = max(letter_count.values())
    result = {key: value for key, value in letter_count.items() if value == max_count}
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The input string should be split by space to get individual letters.
2. Create a dictionary to store the count of each letter.
3. Iterate through the letters and update the count in the dictionary.
4. Find the maximum count value in the dictionary. If the dictionary is empty, handle this case to avoid the `ValueError`.
5. Create a new dictionary containing only the letters with the maximum count value.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def histogram(test):
    letters = test.split()
    letter_count = {}
    
    for letter in letters:
        if letter in letter_count:
            letter_count[letter] += 1
        else:
            letter_count[letter] = 1
    
    if not letter_count:  # Check if the dictionary is empty
        return {}
    
    max_count = max(letter_count.values())
    result = {key: value for key, value in letter_count.items() if value == max_count}
    
    return result
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Remove all characters in string `s` that are present in string `c`.
2. Check if the resulting string is a palindrome by comparing the string with its reverse.
3. Return a tuple containing the resulting string and a boolean indicating whether it is a palindrome.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def reverse_delete(s, c):
    # Remove characters in s that are present in c
    result_string = ''.join([char for char in s if char not in c])
    
    # Check if the result_string is a palindrome
    is_palindrome = result_string == result_string[::-1]
    
    return result_string, is_palindrome
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Iterate through each string in the input list.
2. For each string, count the number of odd digits present.
3. Replace the 'i's in the output string with the count of odd digits for that string.
4. Append the modified strings to a new list.
5. Return the new list of modified strings.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def odd_count(lst):
    def count_odd_digits(s):
        return sum(int(d) % 2 != 0 for d in s)
    
    output = []
    for string in lst:
        count = count_odd_digits(string)
        modified_string = f"the number of odd elements {count}n the str{count}ng {string} of the {count}nput."
        output.append(modified_string)
    
    return output
```

```python
def odd_count(lst):
    def count_odd_digits(s):
        return sum(int(d) % 2 != 0 for d in s)
    
    output = []
    for index, string in enumerate(lst):  # Using enumerate to get the index of each string
        count = count_odd_digits(string)
        modified_string = f"the number of odd elements {count}n the str{index}ng {string} of the {index}nput."  # Replacing 'i' with 'index'
        output.append(modified_string)
    
    return output
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to find the minimum sum of any non-empty sub-array within the given array of integers.
2. Initialize variables `min_sum` to store the minimum sum found so far and `current_sum` to store the sum of the current sub-array.
3. Initialize `min_sum` with a large value or float('inf') to ensure any sum calculated will be less than this initial value.
4. Iterate over the array and for each element, update the `current_sum` by adding the element to it.
5. If the `current_sum` becomes less than the `min_sum`, update the `min_sum` to be equal to `current_sum`.
6. If the `current_sum` becomes negative, reset it to 0 as we are looking for non-empty sub-arrays.
7. Continue this process for all elements in the array.
8. Finally, return the `min_sum` as the result.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def minSubArraySum(nums):
    min_sum = float('inf')
    current_sum = 0
    
    for num in nums:
        current_sum += num
        if current_sum < min_sum:
            min_sum = current_sum
        if current_sum < 0:
            current_sum = 0
    
    return min_sum
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to iterate over each well in the grid and calculate the amount of water in each well.
2. We will keep extracting water from the well until it becomes empty.
3. We will keep track of the total number of times we need to lower the buckets.
4. Finally, we will return the total count of lowering the buckets.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def max_fill(grid, capacity):
    total_lowering = 0
    for row in grid:
        for well in row:
            while well > 0:
                well -= min(well, capacity)
                total_lowering += 1
    return total_lowering
```

### Code
```python
import math

def max_fill(grid, capacity):
    total_lowering = 0
    for row in grid:
        for well in row:
            while well > 0:
                well -= min(well, capacity)
                total_lowering += 1
    return total_lowering
```

```
import math

def max_fill(grid, capacity):
    total_lowering = 0
    for row in grid:
        for well in row:
            while well > 0:
                well -= capacity
                total_lowering += 1
    return total_lowering
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The error occurred because the logic to extract water from each well is not implemented correctly.
2. The current implementation subtracts the bucket capacity directly from the well value, which is incorrect.
3. We need to adjust the logic to correctly extract water from each well based on the bucket capacity.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def max_fill(grid, capacity):
    total_lowering = 0
    for row in grid:
        for well in row:
            while well > 0:
                well -= min(well, capacity)  # Corrected logic to extract water based on bucket capacity
                total_lowering += 1
    return total_lowering
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We need to sort the given array of non-negative integers based on the number of ones in their binary representation in ascending order.
2. If two numbers have the same number of ones in their binary representation, we need to sort them based on their decimal value.
3. We will first define a custom sorting key function that calculates the number of ones in the binary representation of a number and then the number itself.
4. We will use this custom sorting key function with the `sorted` function to sort the array.
5. We will return the sorted array.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def sort_array(arr):
    def custom_sort_key(num):
        return bin(num).count('1'), num

    return sorted(arr, key=custom_sort_key)
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Split the input string `s` into individual words.
2. For each word, count the number of consonants in it.
3. If the count of consonants in a word is equal to the input `n`, then add that word to the result list.
4. Return the list of words that contain exactly `n` consonants.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def select_words(s, n):
    def count_consonants(word):
        consonants = set("bcdfghjklmnpqrstvwxyzBCDFGHJKLMNPQRSTVWXYZ")
        return sum(1 for letter in word if letter in consonants)

    words = s.split()
    result = [word for word in words if count_consonants(word) == n]
    return result
```

```python
    vowels = set('aeiouAEIOU')

    return ""
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. We are given a list of two strings containing only open and close parentheses.
2. We need to check if it is possible to concatenate the two strings in some order to form a good string with balanced parentheses.
3. To achieve this, we can simulate the scenario by tracking the count of open and close parentheses.
4. We can iterate through both strings and increment the count for open parentheses and decrement the count for close parentheses.
5. If at any point the count of close parentheses becomes negative or the final count is not zero, then the resulting string will not have balanced parentheses.
6. If the count remains non-negative and becomes zero at the end of both strings, then the resulting string will have balanced parentheses.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def match_parens(lst):
    open_count = 0
    close_count = 0

    for s in lst:
        for char in s:
            if char == '(':
                open_count += 1
            elif char == ')':
                if open_count > 0:
                    open_count -= 1
                else:
                    close_count += 1

    if open_count == 0 and close_count == 0:
        return 'Yes'
    else:
        return 'No'
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. To solve this problem, we need to find the k maximum elements in the given array `arr`.
2. We can achieve this by sorting the array in descending order and returning the first k elements.
3. If k is 0, we can simply return an empty list.
4. If k is equal to the length of the array, we can return the sorted array as the output.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def maximum(arr, k):
    if k == 0:
        return []
    
    sorted_arr = sorted(arr, reverse=True)
    
    return sorted_arr[:k]
```

```python

    total_sum = 0
    
    for i in range(k):
        if abs(arr[i]) < 100:
            total_sum += arr[i]
    
    return total_sum
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Initialize an empty list to store the odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.
2. Start with the given positive integer `n`.
3. Calculate the Collatz sequence for `n` by following the rules:
    - If the number is even, divide it by 2.
    - If the number is odd, multiply it by 3 and add 1.
4. Keep applying the rules until the sequence reaches 1.
5. While calculating the sequence, store only the odd numbers in the list.
6. Sort the list of odd numbers in increasing order.
7. Return the sorted list of odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    odd_collatz = []
    
    while n != 1:
        if n % 2 == 0:
            n = n // 2
        else:
            odd_collatz.append(n)
            n = 3 * n + 1
    
    odd_collatz.append(1)  # Adding 1 to the list as it's always present in the Collatz sequence
    return sorted(odd_collatz)
```

1. First, we need to check if the input date string is not empty.
2. Then, we need to split the date string into day, month, and year.
3. Next, we need to check if the month is between 1 and 12.
4. Depending on the month, we need to validate the number of days:
    - For months 1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, and 12, the number of days should be between 1 and 31.
    - For months 4, 6, 9, and 11, the number of days should be between 1 and 30.
    - For February (month 2), the number of days should be between 1 and 29.
5. If all these conditions are satisfied, return True. Otherwise, return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```python
def valid_date(date):
    if not date:
        return False
    
    date_parts = date.split('-')
    if len(date_parts) != 3:
        return False
    
    month = int(date_parts[0])
    day = int(date_parts[1])
    year = int(date_parts[2])
    
    if month < 1 or month > 12:
        return False
    
    if month in [1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12] and (day < 1 or day > 31):
        return False
    elif month in [4, 6, 9, 11] and (day < 1 or day > 30):
        return False
    elif month == 2 and (day < 1 or day > 29):
        return False
    
    return True
```

```python
def split_words(txt):
    if ' ' in txt:
        return txt.split()
    elif ',' in txt:
        return txt.split(',')
    else:
        count = sum(1 for c in txt if c.islower() and ord(c) % 2 != 0)
        return count
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. Check if the input list is empty or has only one element, return True as it is considered sorted.
2. Iterate through the list comparing each element with the next element.
3. If the next element is less than the current element or if there are more than 1 duplicates of the same number, return False.
4. If the loop completes without returning False, return True indicating that the list is sorted.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def is_sorted(lst):
    if len(lst) <= 1:
        return True
    
    prev_num = lst[0]
    duplicate_count = 0
    
    for num in lst[1:]:
        if num < prev_num or num == prev_num:
            return False
        prev_num = num
    
    return True
```

[31m[GPT-3.5-TURBO][0m ### Step-by-step reasoning
1. The error occurred due to the logic error in the comparison condition within the for loop.
2. The condition `num == prev_num` should not be used to check for duplicates as it does not capture the requirement correctly.
3. To check for duplicates, we need to keep track of the previous number and the count of duplicates of that number.
4. If a number is encountered that is equal to the previous number, we increment the duplicate count.
5. If the duplicate count exceeds 1, we return False.

### Requirements
None

### Code
```
def is_sorted(lst):
    if len(lst) <= 1:
        return True
    
    prev_num = lst[0]
    duplicate_count = 0
    
    for num in lst[1:]:
        if num < prev_num or (num == prev_num and duplicate_count > 0):
            return False
        elif num == prev_num:
            duplicate_count += 1
        else:
            duplicate_count = 0
        prev_num = num
    
    return True
```
